From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I'm here because I've been really bothered by this scar on my wrist. I mean, logically, I know it's probably not as bad as it looks to me. But lately, when I’m out doing things, like at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. It feels like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now, and it's just getting hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the scar on your wrist that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about people noticing?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the scar. I don't... I don't have anything else I'm worried about people looking at. I mean, I have the usual complaints for someone my age, I suppose, but nothing that actually bothers me. It's just this one spot on my wrist.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about the scar on your wrist been affecting your daily life or activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it actually has. I do bookkeeping work, and I've been late turning some of it in recently. I've also skipped a few of my volunteer commitments—I help out with a choir and a local theater group. It’s just... I get so caught up trying to figure out how to hide it or make sure my wrist won't be noticed, that it just gets in the way of getting my work done or going out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your scar is as noticeable to others as it is to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned when we first started talking, logically I know it's probably not. I know it's likely not as bad to other people as it looks to me. But... when I'm actually out and about, you know, sitting around the bridge table or at church, I just can't shake the feeling that everyone's eyes go right to it. It just feels like it's the very first thing they notice about me, even if my rational brain tells me they probably aren't looking at it at all.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar more about how it looks or about what it might represent to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it quite like that. I mean, I don't know if I'm worried about what it represents to people. I just... I really don't like the way it looks. It's just ugly to me, and I hate the idea of people staring at it. I haven't really considered if they think it means something specific, I just want it covered up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually notice your scar and stare at it, or is it more that you just feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I guess I'm not entirely sure. I mean, I know I'm definitely self-conscious about it. But when I'm out, it really does feel like people are looking at it. I haven't exactly caught anyone staring or pointing at it, but the feeling that they are is just so strong. So, I don't know for a fact that they notice it, but it certainly feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar would decrease if you could somehow make it less noticeable to others, without actually removing or changing the scar itself?\n\nPatient: I suppose it would, because... well, that's actually what I try to do right now. I mean, before I leave the house, I’ll usually check my wrist in the hall mirror a few times. I try on different sleeves or put on a bracelet just to cover it up and make it less visible. I even ask my husband sometimes if it looks obvious, even though he always says it’s barely there. So, doing those things to hide it does help me feel a little better about going out, but the worry doesn't completely go away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don't... I don't have anything else I'm worried about people looking at. I mean, I have the usual complaints for someone my age, I suppose, but nothing that actually bothers me. It's just this one spot on my wrist.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about the scar on your wrist been affecting your daily life or activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it actually has. I do bookkeeping work, and I've been late turning some of it in recently. I've also skipped a few of my volunteer commitments—I help out with a choir and a local theater group. It’s just... I get so caught up trying to figure out how to hide it or make sure my wrist won't be noticed, that it just gets in the way of getting my work done or going out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your scar is as noticeable to others as it is to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned when we first started talking, logically I know it's probably not. I know it's likely not as bad to other people as it looks to me. But... when I'm actually out and about, you know, sitting around the bridge table or at church, I just can't shake the feeling that everyone's eyes go right to it. It just feels like it's the very first thing they notice about me, even if my rational brain tells me they probably aren't looking at it at all.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar more about how it looks or about what it might represent to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it quite like that. I mean, I don't know if I'm worried about what it represents to people. I just... I really don't like the way it looks. It's just ugly to me, and I hate the idea of people staring at it. I haven't really considered if they think it means something specific, I just want it covered up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually notice your scar and stare at it, or is it more that you just feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I guess I'm not entirely sure. I mean, I know I'm definitely self-conscious about it. But when I'm out, it really does feel like people are looking at it. I haven't exactly caught anyone staring or pointing at it, but the feeling that they are is just so strong. So, I don't know for a fact that they notice it, but it certainly feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar would decrease if you could somehow make it less noticeable to others, without actually removing or changing the scar itself?\n\nPatient: I suppose it would, because... well, that's actually what I try to do right now. I mean, before I leave the house, I’ll usually check my wrist in the hall mirror a few times. I try on different sleeves or put on a bracelet just to cover it up and make it less visible. I even ask my husband sometimes if it looks obvious, even though he always says it’s barely there. So, doing those things to hide it does help me feel a little better about going out, but the worry doesn't completely go away.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar mostly about its appearance or about what people might think it means about you?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me something similar earlier. It's really just about how it looks. I haven't thought about people thinking it means anything specific about me. I just think it looks bad, and I don't want anyone to see it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I'm here because I've been really bothered by this scar on my wrist. I mean, logically, I know it's probably not as bad as it looks to me. But lately, when I’m out doing things, like at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. It feels like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now, and it's just getting hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the scar on your wrist that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about people noticing?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the scar. I don't... I don't have anything else I'm worried about people looking at. I mean, I have the usual complaints for someone my age, I suppose, but nothing that actually bothers me. It's just this one spot on my wrist.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about the scar on your wrist been affecting your daily life or activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it actually has. I do bookkeeping work, and I've been late turning some of it in recently. I've also skipped a few of my volunteer commitments—I help out with a choir and a local theater group. It’s just... I get so caught up trying to figure out how to hide it or make sure my wrist won't be noticed, that it just gets in the way of getting my work done or going out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your scar is as noticeable to others as it is to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned when we first started talking, logically I know it's probably not. I know it's likely not as bad to other people as it looks to me. But... when I'm actually out and about, you know, sitting around the bridge table or at church, I just can't shake the feeling that everyone's eyes go right to it. It just feels like it's the very first thing they notice about me, even if my rational brain tells me they probably aren't looking at it at all.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar more about how it looks or about what it might represent to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it quite like that. I mean, I don't know if I'm worried about what it represents to people. I just... I really don't like the way it looks. It's just ugly to me, and I hate the idea of people staring at it. I haven't really considered if they think it means something specific, I just want it covered up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually notice your scar and stare at it, or is it more that you just feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I guess I'm not entirely sure. I mean, I know I'm definitely self-conscious about it. But when I'm out, it really does feel like people are looking at it. I haven't exactly caught anyone staring or pointing at it, but the feeling that they are is just so strong. So, I don't know for a fact that they notice it, but it certainly feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar would decrease if you could somehow make it less noticeable to others, without actually removing or changing the scar itself?\n\nPatient: I suppose it would, because... well, that's actually what I try to do right now. I mean, before I leave the house, I’ll usually check my wrist in the hall mirror a few times. I try on different sleeves or put on a bracelet just to cover it up and make it less visible. I even ask my husband sometimes if it looks obvious, even though he always says it’s barely there. So, doing those things to hide it does help me feel a little better about going out, but the worry doesn't completely go away.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar mostly about its appearance or about what people might think it means about you?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me something similar earlier. It's really just about how it looks. I haven't thought about people thinking it means anything specific about me. I just think it looks bad, and I don't want anyone to see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that everyone notices your scar is based on anything people have actually said or done?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, no one has ever actually said anything to me about it. And like I mentioned before, I haven't caught anyone pointing or staring. It's just a feeling I get when I'm around other people. I just assume they can see it because it feels so obvious to me. But no, it's not based on anyone actually saying or doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the scar repeatedly throughout the day, even when you're not in situations where it might be seen by others?\n\nPatient: Well, if I'm just at home by myself, I don't dwell on it quite as much. It crosses my mind, but... the real problem is when I'm out. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church, or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. It feels like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. So that's when I really can't stop thinking about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I'm here because I've been really bothered by this scar on my wrist. I mean, logically, I know it's probably not as bad as it looks to me. But lately, when I’m out doing things, like at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. It feels like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now, and it's just getting hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the scar on your wrist that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about people noticing?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the scar. I don't... I don't have anything else I'm worried about people looking at. I mean, I have the usual complaints for someone my age, I suppose, but nothing that actually bothers me. It's just this one spot on my wrist.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about the scar on your wrist been affecting your daily life or activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it actually has. I do bookkeeping work, and I've been late turning some of it in recently. I've also skipped a few of my volunteer commitments—I help out with a choir and a local theater group. It’s just... I get so caught up trying to figure out how to hide it or make sure my wrist won't be noticed, that it just gets in the way of getting my work done or going out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your scar is as noticeable to others as it is to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned when we first started talking, logically I know it's probably not. I know it's likely not as bad to other people as it looks to me. But... when I'm actually out and about, you know, sitting around the bridge table or at church, I just can't shake the feeling that everyone's eyes go right to it. It just feels like it's the very first thing they notice about me, even if my rational brain tells me they probably aren't looking at it at all.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar more about how it looks or about what it might represent to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it quite like that. I mean, I don't know if I'm worried about what it represents to people. I just... I really don't like the way it looks. It's just ugly to me, and I hate the idea of people staring at it. I haven't really considered if they think it means something specific, I just want it covered up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually notice your scar and stare at it, or is it more that you just feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I guess I'm not entirely sure. I mean, I know I'm definitely self-conscious about it. But when I'm out, it really does feel like people are looking at it. I haven't exactly caught anyone staring or pointing at it, but the feeling that they are is just so strong. So, I don't know for a fact that they notice it, but it certainly feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar would decrease if you could somehow make it less noticeable to others, without actually removing or changing the scar itself?\n\nPatient: I suppose it would, because... well, that's actually what I try to do right now. I mean, before I leave the house, I’ll usually check my wrist in the hall mirror a few times. I try on different sleeves or put on a bracelet just to cover it up and make it less visible. I even ask my husband sometimes if it looks obvious, even though he always says it’s barely there. So, doing those things to hide it does help me feel a little better about going out, but the worry doesn't completely go away.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar mostly about its appearance or about what people might think it means about you?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me something similar earlier. It's really just about how it looks. I haven't thought about people thinking it means anything specific about me. I just think it looks bad, and I don't want anyone to see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that everyone notices your scar is based on anything people have actually said or done?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, no one has ever actually said anything to me about it. And like I mentioned before, I haven't caught anyone pointing or staring. It's just a feeling I get when I'm around other people. I just assume they can see it because it feels so obvious to me. But no, it's not based on anyone actually saying or doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the scar repeatedly throughout the day, even when you're not in situations where it might be seen by others?\n\nPatient: Well, if I'm just at home by myself, I don't dwell on it quite as much. It crosses my mind, but... the real problem is when I'm out. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church, or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. It feels like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. So that's when I really can't stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your reaction to the scar is stronger than what most people would experience if they had a similar scar?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I don't know how other people would react to having this on their wrist. I just know how it makes me feel. I suppose some people might not care as much, but to me, it's just very bothersome when I'm around others.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I'm here because I've been really bothered by this scar on my wrist. I mean, logically, I know it's probably not as bad as it looks to me. But lately, when I’m out doing things, like at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. It feels like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now, and it's just getting hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the scar on your wrist that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about people noticing?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the scar. I don't... I don't have anything else I'm worried about people looking at. I mean, I have the usual complaints for someone my age, I suppose, but nothing that actually bothers me. It's just this one spot on my wrist.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about the scar on your wrist been affecting your daily life or activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it actually has. I do bookkeeping work, and I've been late turning some of it in recently. I've also skipped a few of my volunteer commitments—I help out with a choir and a local theater group. It’s just... I get so caught up trying to figure out how to hide it or make sure my wrist won't be noticed, that it just gets in the way of getting my work done or going out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your scar is as noticeable to others as it is to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned when we first started talking, logically I know it's probably not. I know it's likely not as bad to other people as it looks to me. But... when I'm actually out and about, you know, sitting around the bridge table or at church, I just can't shake the feeling that everyone's eyes go right to it. It just feels like it's the very first thing they notice about me, even if my rational brain tells me they probably aren't looking at it at all.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar more about how it looks or about what it might represent to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it quite like that. I mean, I don't know if I'm worried about what it represents to people. I just... I really don't like the way it looks. It's just ugly to me, and I hate the idea of people staring at it. I haven't really considered if they think it means something specific, I just want it covered up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually notice your scar and stare at it, or is it more that you just feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I guess I'm not entirely sure. I mean, I know I'm definitely self-conscious about it. But when I'm out, it really does feel like people are looking at it. I haven't exactly caught anyone staring or pointing at it, but the feeling that they are is just so strong. So, I don't know for a fact that they notice it, but it certainly feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar would decrease if you could somehow make it less noticeable to others, without actually removing or changing the scar itself?\n\nPatient: I suppose it would, because... well, that's actually what I try to do right now. I mean, before I leave the house, I’ll usually check my wrist in the hall mirror a few times. I try on different sleeves or put on a bracelet just to cover it up and make it less visible. I even ask my husband sometimes if it looks obvious, even though he always says it’s barely there. So, doing those things to hide it does help me feel a little better about going out, but the worry doesn't completely go away.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar mostly about its appearance or about what people might think it means about you?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me something similar earlier. It's really just about how it looks. I haven't thought about people thinking it means anything specific about me. I just think it looks bad, and I don't want anyone to see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that everyone notices your scar is based on anything people have actually said or done?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, no one has ever actually said anything to me about it. And like I mentioned before, I haven't caught anyone pointing or staring. It's just a feeling I get when I'm around other people. I just assume they can see it because it feels so obvious to me. But no, it's not based on anyone actually saying or doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the scar repeatedly throughout the day, even when you're not in situations where it might be seen by others?\n\nPatient: Well, if I'm just at home by myself, I don't dwell on it quite as much. It crosses my mind, but... the real problem is when I'm out. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church, or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. It feels like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. So that's when I really can't stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your reaction to the scar is stronger than what most people would experience if they had a similar scar?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I don't know how other people would react to having this on their wrist. I just know how it makes me feel. I suppose some people might not care as much, but to me, it's just very bothersome when I'm around others.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your reaction to the scar would be less intense if you could see it from an outsider's perspective?\n\nPatient: I'm... I'm really not sure. It's hard to say. I guess I just can't quite imagine looking at it through someone else's eyes. I only know what I see when I look down at my own wrist, so... I don't really know if it would change how intense it feels for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I'm here because I've been really bothered by this scar on my wrist. I mean, logically, I know it's probably not as bad as it looks to me. But lately, when I’m out doing things, like at church or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. It feels like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now, and it's just getting hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the scar on your wrist that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about people noticing?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the scar. I don't... I don't have anything else I'm worried about people looking at. I mean, I have the usual complaints for someone my age, I suppose, but nothing that actually bothers me. It's just this one spot on my wrist.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about the scar on your wrist been affecting your daily life or activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it actually has. I do bookkeeping work, and I've been late turning some of it in recently. I've also skipped a few of my volunteer commitments—I help out with a choir and a local theater group. It’s just... I get so caught up trying to figure out how to hide it or make sure my wrist won't be noticed, that it just gets in the way of getting my work done or going out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your scar is as noticeable to others as it is to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned when we first started talking, logically I know it's probably not. I know it's likely not as bad to other people as it looks to me. But... when I'm actually out and about, you know, sitting around the bridge table or at church, I just can't shake the feeling that everyone's eyes go right to it. It just feels like it's the very first thing they notice about me, even if my rational brain tells me they probably aren't looking at it at all.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar more about how it looks or about what it might represent to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it quite like that. I mean, I don't know if I'm worried about what it represents to people. I just... I really don't like the way it looks. It's just ugly to me, and I hate the idea of people staring at it. I haven't really considered if they think it means something specific, I just want it covered up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually notice your scar and stare at it, or is it more that you just feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I guess I'm not entirely sure. I mean, I know I'm definitely self-conscious about it. But when I'm out, it really does feel like people are looking at it. I haven't exactly caught anyone staring or pointing at it, but the feeling that they are is just so strong. So, I don't know for a fact that they notice it, but it certainly feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar would decrease if you could somehow make it less noticeable to others, without actually removing or changing the scar itself?\n\nPatient: I suppose it would, because... well, that's actually what I try to do right now. I mean, before I leave the house, I’ll usually check my wrist in the hall mirror a few times. I try on different sleeves or put on a bracelet just to cover it up and make it less visible. I even ask my husband sometimes if it looks obvious, even though he always says it’s barely there. So, doing those things to hide it does help me feel a little better about going out, but the worry doesn't completely go away.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar mostly about its appearance or about what people might think it means about you?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me something similar earlier. It's really just about how it looks. I haven't thought about people thinking it means anything specific about me. I just think it looks bad, and I don't want anyone to see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that everyone notices your scar is based on anything people have actually said or done?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, no one has ever actually said anything to me about it. And like I mentioned before, I haven't caught anyone pointing or staring. It's just a feeling I get when I'm around other people. I just assume they can see it because it feels so obvious to me. But no, it's not based on anyone actually saying or doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the scar repeatedly throughout the day, even when you're not in situations where it might be seen by others?\n\nPatient: Well, if I'm just at home by myself, I don't dwell on it quite as much. It crosses my mind, but... the real problem is when I'm out. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church, or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. It feels like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. So that's when I really can't stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your reaction to the scar is stronger than what most people would experience if they had a similar scar?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I don't know how other people would react to having this on their wrist. I just know how it makes me feel. I suppose some people might not care as much, but to me, it's just very bothersome when I'm around others.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your reaction to the scar would be less intense if you could see it from an outsider's perspective?\n\nPatient: I'm... I'm really not sure. It's hard to say. I guess I just can't quite imagine looking at it through someone else's eyes. I only know what I see when I look down at my own wrist, so... I don't really know if it would change how intense it feels for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your distress about the scar is proportional to how it actually looks?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean... I know it's just a scar, but it feels like a big deal to me. I can't really say if my reaction is proportional or not, because I only know how much it bothers me when I'm trying to go about my day.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry about the scar affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Well, it has affected things, yes. I mean... I've actually been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work lately. And I've skipped out on a few things with friends, like my choir and theater volunteer commitments. I just... I get so caught up trying to make sure my wrist won't be noticed by anyone that it makes me fall behind, or I just stay home instead of going out to see people.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It feels like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now, and it's just getting hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the scar on your wrist that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about people noticing?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the scar. I don't... I don't have anything else I'm worried about people looking at. I mean, I have the usual complaints for someone my age, I suppose, but nothing that actually bothers me. I do bookkeeping work, and I've been late turning some of it in recently. I've also skipped a few of my volunteer commitments—I help out with a choir and a local theater group. It’s just... I get so caught up trying to figure out how to hide it or make sure my wrist won't be noticed, that it just gets in the way of getting my work done or going out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your scar is as noticeable to others as it is to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned when we first started talking, logically I know it's probably not. I know it's likely not as bad to other people as it looks to me. But... when I'm actually out and about, you know, sitting around the bridge table or at church, I just can't shake the feeling that everyone's eyes go right to it. It just feels like it's the very first thing they notice about me, even if my rational brain tells me they probably aren't looking at it at all.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar more about how it looks or about what it might represent to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it quite like that. I mean, I don't know if I'm worried about what it represents to people. I just... I really don't like the way it looks. It's just ugly to me, and I hate the idea of people staring at it. I haven't really considered if they think it means something specific, I just want it covered up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually notice your scar and stare at it, or is it more that you just feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I guess I'm not entirely sure. I mean, I know I'm definitely self-conscious about it. But when I'm out, it really does feel like people are looking at it. I haven't exactly caught anyone staring or pointing at it, but the feeling that they are is just so strong. So, I don't know for a fact that they notice it, but it certainly feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar would decrease if you could somehow make it less noticeable to others, without actually removing or changing the scar itself?\n\nPatient: I suppose it would, because... well, that's actually what I try to do right now. I mean, before I leave the house, I’ll usually check my wrist in the hall mirror a few times. I try on different sleeves or put on a bracelet just to cover it up and make it less visible. I even ask my husband sometimes if it looks obvious, even though he always says it’s barely there. So, doing those things to hide it does help me feel a little better about going out, but the worry doesn't completely go away.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar mostly about its appearance or about what people might think it means about you?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me something similar earlier. It's really just about how it looks. I haven't thought about people thinking it means anything specific about me. I just think it looks bad, and I don't want anyone to see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that everyone notices your scar is based on anything people have actually said or done?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, no one has ever actually said anything to me about it. And like I mentioned before, I haven't caught anyone pointing or staring. It's just a feeling I get when I'm around other people. I just assume they can see it because it feels so obvious to me. It feels like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. So that's when I really can't stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your reaction to the scar is stronger than what most people would experience if they had a similar scar?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I don't know how other people would react to having this on their wrist. I just know how it makes me feel. I suppose some people might not care as much, but to me, it's just very bothersome when I'm around others.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your reaction to the scar would be less intense if you could see it from an outsider's perspective?\n\nPatient: I'm... I'm really not sure. It's hard to say. I guess I just can't quite imagine looking at it through someone else's eyes. I only know what I see when I look down at my own wrist, so... I don't really know if it would change how intense it feels for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your distress about the scar is proportional to how it actually looks?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I don't... I don't have anything else I'm worried about people looking at. I mean, I have the usual complaints for someone my age, I suppose, but nothing that actually bothers me. It's just this one spot on my wrist.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about the scar on your wrist been affecting your daily life or activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it actually has. I do bookkeeping work, and I've been late turning some of it in recently. I've also skipped a few of my volunteer commitments—I help out with a choir and a local theater group. It’s just... I get so caught up trying to figure out how to hide it or make sure my wrist won't be noticed, that it just gets in the way of getting my work done or going out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your scar is as noticeable to others as it is to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned when we first started talking, logically I know it's probably not. I know it's likely not as bad to other people as it looks to me. But... when I'm actually out and about, you know, sitting around the bridge table or at church, I just can't shake the feeling that everyone's eyes go right to it. It just feels like it's the very first thing they notice about me, even if my rational brain tells me they probably aren't looking at it at all.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar more about how it looks or about what it might represent to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it quite like that. I mean, I don't know if I'm worried about what it represents to people. I just... I really don't like the way it looks. It's just ugly to me, and I hate the idea of people staring at it. I haven't really considered if they think it means something specific, I just want it covered up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually notice your scar and stare at it, or is it more that you just feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I guess I'm not entirely sure. I mean, I know I'm definitely self-conscious about it. But when I'm out, it really does feel like people are looking at it. I haven't exactly caught anyone staring or pointing at it, but the feeling that they are is just so strong. So, I don't know for a fact that they notice it, but it certainly feels that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the scar would decrease if you could somehow make it less noticeable to others, without actually removing or changing the scar itself?\n\nPatient: I suppose it would, because... well, that's actually what I try to do right now. I mean, before I leave the house, I’ll usually check my wrist in the hall mirror a few times. I try on different sleeves or put on a bracelet just to cover it up and make it less visible. I even ask my husband sometimes if it looks obvious, even though he always says it’s barely there. So, doing those things to hide it does help me feel a little better about going out, but the worry doesn't completely go away.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar mostly about its appearance or about what people might think it means about you?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me something similar earlier. It's really just about how it looks. I haven't thought about people thinking it means anything specific about me. I just think it looks bad, and I don't want anyone to see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that everyone notices your scar is based on anything people have actually said or done?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, no one has ever actually said anything to me about it. And like I mentioned before, I haven't caught anyone pointing or staring. It's just a feeling I get when I'm around other people. I just assume they can see it because it feels so obvious to me. But no, it's not based on anyone actually saying or doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the scar repeatedly throughout the day, even when you're not in situations where it might be seen by others?\n\nPatient: Well, if I'm just at home by myself, I don't dwell on it quite as much. It crosses my mind, but... the real problem is when I'm out. I mean, I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church, or playing bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. It feels like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. So that's when I really can't stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your reaction to the scar is stronger than what most people would experience if they had a similar scar?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I don't know how other people would react to having this on their wrist. I just know how it makes me feel. I suppose some people might not care as much, but to me, it's just very bothersome when I'm around others.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your reaction to the scar would be less intense if you could see it from an outsider's perspective?\n\nPatient: I'm... I'm really not sure. It's hard to say. I guess I just can't quite imagine looking at it through someone else's eyes. I only know what I see when I look down at my own wrist, so... I don't really know if it would change how intense it feels for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your distress about the scar is proportional to how it actually looks?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean... I know it's just a scar, but it feels like a big deal to me. I can't really say if my reaction is proportional or not, because I only know how much it bothers me when I'm trying to go about my day.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry about the scar affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Well, it has affected things, yes. I mean... I've actually been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work lately. And I've skipped out on a few things with friends, like my choir and theater volunteer commitments. I just... I get so caught up trying to make sure my wrist won't be noticed by anyone that it makes me fall behind, or I just stay home instead of going out to see people.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social interactions or activities that you would normally enjoy because of your concern about the scar?\n\nPatient: Yes, like I was just mentioning, that's exactly what's been happening. I normally really enjoy my choir group and volunteering at the theater, but I've been skipping them lately. It's just... I spend so much time worrying about my wrist and trying to make sure nobody notices it that it's easier to just not go. And it's the same reason I've been turning in my bookkeeping work late, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry about the scar is interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that are important to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is. I know we just touched on this, but... yes, definitely. Those choir and theater groups are very important to me, and I'm just not participating in them right now. And it's interfering with my job, too, since my bookkeeping work is getting turned in late. It all just comes back to me getting so caught up in trying to hide my wrist that it gets in the way of the things I actually want or need to be doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
